🌐 HTML MASTERY — COMPLETE STUDY GUIDE
Beginner → Advanced | Concepts + Code + Diagrams + Projects + Interview Questions
HTML is the foundation of every web page. If you want to become a Full-Stack, AI/ML, or web developer, HTML should be extremely strong.

🧠 Master formula:
HTML = Structure + Meaning + Content

Think of a website like a human body:

                 🌐 WEBSITE
                     │
          ┌──────────┴──────────┐
          │                     │
        HTML                  CSS
          │                     │
      Structure              Design
          │                     │
       "What?"              "How?"
          │
          ▼
    ┌──────────────┐
    │ Heading      │
    │ Paragraph    │
    │ Image        │
    │ Form         │
    │ Button       │
    │ Table        │
    └──────────────┘
🧠 1. What is HTML?
HTML = HyperText Markup Language
Break the difficult words:

Word	Easy meaning
HyperText	Text connected to other pages
Markup	Special tags that describe content
Language	Rules used to write something
Simple definition
HTML is a markup language used to create the structure and content of web pages.

HTML is not a programming language because it does not normally contain programming logic like loops, conditions, or functions.

🎯 2. HTML vs CSS vs JavaScript
This is one of the most important concepts.

              WEB PAGE
                 │
      ┌──────────┼──────────┐
      ▼          ▼          ▼
    HTML        CSS      JavaScript
      │          │          │
 Structure     Design      Behavior
      │          │          │
   What?       How?       Action?
Example:

<button>Click Me</button>
HTML creates the button.

button {
    color: white;
    background: blue;
}
CSS designs it.

button.addEventListener("click", function () {
    alert("Hello!");
});
JavaScript gives it behavior.

🧠 Remember
HTML = Skeleton
CSS = Clothes/Design
JavaScript = Brain/Behavior

🟢 4. Basic HTML Document
Every beginner should memorize this structure.

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html lang="en">

<head>
    <meta charset="UTF-8">

    <meta
        name="viewport"
        content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
    >

    <title>My Website</title>
</head>

<body>

    <h1>Hello World</h1>

    <p>Welcome to my website.</p>

</body>

</html>
🔍 5. Understand Every Line
<!DOCTYPE html>
Tells the browser:

"This document uses modern HTML."

<html>
Root element.

<html>
   │
   ├── head
   │
   └── body
</html>
<head>
Contains information about the page.

Examples:

<head>

    <title>My Page</title>

    <meta charset="UTF-8">

    <meta
        name="description"
        content="My personal portfolio"
    >

</head>
<body>
Contains what the user sees.

<body>

    <h1>Hello</h1>

    <p>This is visible.</p>

</body>
🧩 6. Tag vs Element vs Attribute
Very important.

<p class="intro">Hello World</p>
Breakdown:

<p class="intro">Hello World</p>
│ │       │        │        │
│ │       │        │        └── Closing tag
│ │       │        └────────── Content
│ │       └────────────────── Attribute value
│ └────────────────────────── Attribute
└──────────────────────────── Tag
Element
The complete structure:

<p class="intro">Hello World</p>
Tag
<p>
</p>
Attribute
class="intro"
🧠 Remember
Tag = instruction
Attribute = extra information
Element = complete HTML unit

🟢 7. Opening and Closing Tags
Normal element:

<h1>Hello</h1>
Opening:

<h1>
Closing:

</h1>
Content:

Hello
⚡ 8. Void Elements
Some HTML elements don't have closing tags.

Examples:

<img>
<br>
<hr>
<input>
<meta>
<link>
Example:

<img src="photo.jpg" alt="Profile photo">
📝 9. Comments
Comments are ignored by the browser.

<!-- This is a comment -->
Example:

<!-- User profile section -->

<h2>Profile</h2>
🏷️ 10. Headings
HTML provides six heading levels.

<h1>Main Heading</h1>
<h2>Section</h2>
<h3>Subsection</h3>
<h4>Small Heading</h4>
<h5>Smaller Heading</h5>
<h6>Smallest Heading</h6>
Hierarchy:

h1
│
├── h2
│   ├── h3
│   └── h3
│
└── h2
    └── h3
🟡 Important
Don't choose heading levels simply because of their visual size.

Use them for document structure.

CSS controls visual size.

📄 11. Paragraph
<p>
    HTML is used to structure web pages.
</p>
Multiple paragraphs:

<p>First paragraph.</p>

<p>Second paragraph.</p>
↩️ 12. Line Break
Hello<br>
World
Result:

Hello
World
⚠️ Don't use <br> for page layout.
Use CSS for layout.

➖ 13. Horizontal Rule
<hr>
Used to represent a thematic separation.

✍️ 14. Text Formatting
Bold importance
<strong>Important</strong>
Emphasis
<em>Important idea</em>
Highlight
<mark>Important</mark>
Small text
<small>Terms and conditions</small>
Deleted text
<del>₹999</del>
Inserted text
<ins>₹799</ins>
Subscript
H<sub>2</sub>O
Superscript
x<sup>2</sup>
🧠 15. <b> vs <strong>
<b>Bold</b>
mainly represents styling/attention.

<strong>Important</strong>
represents strong importance.

Interview answer
<strong> has semantic meaning, while <b> is primarily a stylistic attention element.

🧠 16. <i> vs <em>
<i>Technical term</i>
versus:

<em>Important emphasis</em>
<em> has semantic emphasis.

🔗 17. Links
The anchor element:

<a href="https://example.com">
    Visit Website
</a>
Structure
<a>
 │
 ├── href → destination
 │
 └── text → clickable content
🔗 18. Open Link in New Tab
<a
    href="https://example.com"
    target="_blank"
    rel="noopener noreferrer"
>
    Open Website
</a>
Security note
When using _blank, rel="noopener noreferrer" is a good defensive practice.

📧 19. Email Link
<a href="mailto:aamir@example.com">
    Email Me
</a>
📞 20. Telephone Link
<a href="tel:+911234567890">
    Call Me
</a>
🎯 21. Internal Page Navigation
<a href="#projects">
    Projects
</a>

<section id="projects">

    <h2>Projects</h2>

</section>
Flow:

Click Projects
      ↓
href="#projects"
      ↓
Find id="projects"
      ↓
Scroll to section
🖼️ 22. Images
Basic:

<img
    src="photo.jpg"
    alt="Profile photo"
>
Important attributes
Attribute	Meaning
src	Image location
alt	Alternative text
width	Width
height	Height
loading	Loading behavior
Example:

<img
    src="profile.jpg"
    alt="Aamir profile photo"
    width="300"
    height="300"
    loading="lazy"
>
♿ 23. Why alt is Important
If image cannot load:

Image
 ↓
Cannot load
 ↓
alt text
Screen readers can also use alternative text.

❌ Bad:

<img src="cat.jpg">
✅ Better:

<img
    src="cat.jpg"
    alt="White cat sitting on a chair"
>
📋 24. Unordered List
<ul>

    <li>Python</li>
    <li>SQL</li>
    <li>Machine Learning</li>

</ul>
Result:

• Python
• SQL
• Machine Learning
🔢 25. Ordered List
<ol>

    <li>Learn HTML</li>
    <li>Learn CSS</li>
    <li>Learn JavaScript</li>

</ol>
Result:

1. Learn HTML
2. Learn CSS
3. Learn JavaScript
📚 26. Description List
Useful for terms and definitions.

<dl>

    <dt>HTML</dt>

    <dd>
        Structure of a web page.
    </dd>

    <dt>CSS</dt>

    <dd>
        Styling of a web page.
    </dd>

</dl>
Think:

Term
 ↓
Definition
📊 27. HTML Tables
Basic:

<table>

    <tr>
        <th>Name</th>
        <th>Age</th>
        <th>Role</th>
    </tr>

    <tr>
        <td>Aamir</td>
        <td>24</td>
        <td>AI/ML</td>
    </tr>

</table>
🧩 28. Table Structure
<table>
   │
   ├── <thead>
   │      └── <tr>
   │           ├── <th>
   │           └── <th>
   │
   ├── <tbody>
   │      └── <tr>
   │           ├── <td>
   │           └── <td>
   │
   └── <tfoot>
Professional table:

<table>

    <thead>

        <tr>
            <th>Name</th>
            <th>Skill</th>
        </tr>

    </thead>

    <tbody>

        <tr>
            <td>Aamir</td>
            <td>Python</td>
        </tr>

        <tr>
            <td>Rahul</td>
            <td>Java</td>
        </tr>

    </tbody>

</table>
🔀 29. colspan
Combine columns.

<table>

    <tr>
        <th colspan="2">
            Student Information
        </th>
    </tr>

    <tr>
        <td>Aamir</td>
        <td>AI/ML</td>
    </tr>

</table>
Concept:

Before:

┌────────┬────────┐
│ Name   │ Skill  │
└────────┴────────┘

After colspan:

┌─────────────────┐
│ Student Info    │
└─────────────────┘
↕️ 30. rowspan
Combine rows.

<td rowspan="2">
    AI/ML
</td>
🧱 31. <div>
div is a generic block container.

<div>

    <h2>Profile</h2>

    <p>Hello!</p>

</div>
Think:

<div>
 ├── heading
 ├── paragraph
 └── image
</div>
📝 32. <span>
span is a generic inline container.

<p>
    I love
    <span>Python</span>.
</p>
Difference
div
 ↓
Block-level grouping

span
 ↓
Inline grouping
🧠 33. Semantic HTML
This is very important for interviews.

Semantic elements tell the browser and developer what the content means.

Instead of:

<div class="header">
use:

<header>
Instead of:

<div class="navigation">
use:

<nav>
🗺️ 34. Semantic HTML Diagram
<body>
│
├── <header>
│     └── Logo
│
├── <nav>
│     └── Navigation
│
├── <main>
│     │
│     ├── <section>
│     │
│     ├── <article>
│     │
│     └── <aside>
│
└── <footer>
🏗️ 35. Important Semantic Elements
<header>
Introductory content.

<header>
    <h1>My Portfolio</h1>
</header>
<nav>
Navigation links.

<nav>

    <a href="/">Home</a>
    <a href="#about">About</a>
    <a href="#contact">Contact</a>

</nav>
<main>
Main page content.

<main>

    <h1>AI/ML Engineer</h1>

</main>
A document should normally have one primary <main>.

📦 36. <section>
A thematic section.

<section id="skills">

    <h2>Skills</h2>

    <p>Python, SQL, Machine Learning</p>

</section>
📰 37. <article>
Self-contained content.

Examples:

Blog post
News article
Product card
Forum post
<article>

    <h2>My ML Project</h2>

    <p>
        This project predicts crop yield.
    </p>

</article>
📌 38. <aside>
Related but secondary content.

<aside>

    <h3>Related Projects</h3>

    <p>AI Chatbot</p>

</aside>
🦶 39. <footer>
Bottom/closing information.

<footer>

    <p>
        © 2026 My Portfolio
    </p>

</footer>
📝 40. Forms — Extremely Important
Forms collect user input.

USER
 ↓
FORM
 ↓
INPUT
 ↓
VALIDATION
 ↓
SERVER
 ↓
DATABASE / MODEL
Basic:

<form>

    <label for="name">
        Name
    </label>

    <input
        type="text"
        id="name"
        name="name"
    >

    <button type="submit">
        Submit
    </button>

</form>
🧠 41. Why <label> is Important
<label for="email">
    Email
</label>

<input
    id="email"
    type="email"
>
The for value should match the input's id.

label for="email"
       ↓
input id="email"
Benefits:

Accessibility
Better usability
Clicking label can focus input

HTML provides many input types.

<input type="text">

<input type="password">

<input type="email">

<input type="number">

<input type="date">

<input type="time">

<input type="file">

<input type="checkbox">

<input type="radio">

<input type="range">

<input type="color">

<input type="search">

<input type="tel">

<input type="url">

<input type="hidden">

<input type="submit">
🔐 43. Password Input
<label for="password">
    Password
</label>

<input
    type="password"
    id="password"
    name="password"
>
Important:

type="password" hides characters visually; it does not encrypt the password.

📧 44. Email Input
<input
    type="email"
    name="email"
    required
>
Browser can perform basic validation.

🔢 45. Number Input
<input
    type="number"
    min="18"
    max="100"
>
📅 46. Date
<input
    type="date"
    name="dob"
>
☑️ 47. Checkbox
<label>

    <input
        type="checkbox"
        name="skills"
        value="python"
    >

    Python

</label>
Multiple:

<label>
    <input type="checkbox" name="skills" value="python">
    Python
</label>

<label>
    <input type="checkbox" name="skills" value="sql">
    SQL
</label>

<label>
    <input type="checkbox" name="skills" value="ml">
    Machine Learning
</label>
🔘 48. Radio Buttons
Radio buttons normally allow one selection within the same group.

<label>
    <input
        type="radio"
        name="gender"
        value="male"
    >
    Male
</label>

<label>
    <input
        type="radio"
        name="gender"
        value="female"
    >
    Female
</label>
🧠 Important
Same name:

name="gender"
      ↓
same group
📋 49. Select Dropdown
<label for="city">
    City
</label>

<select id="city" name="city">

    <option value="delhi">
        Delhi
    </option>

    <option value="jaipur">
        Jaipur
    </option>

    <option value="patna">
        Patna
    </option>

</select>
📝 50. Textarea
For long text:

<label for="message">
    Message
</label>

<textarea
    id="message"
    name="message"
    rows="5"
    cols="40"
></textarea>
🔘 51. Button
<button type="button">
    Click
</button>
Submit:

<button type="submit">
    Submit
</button>
Reset:

<button type="reset">
    Reset
</button>
🚨 52. required
<input
    type="text"
    required
>
User must provide a value before submitting.

🔢 53. min, max
<input
    type="number"
    min="1"
    max="100"
>
📏 54. minlength, maxlength
<input
    type="text"
    minlength="3"
    maxlength="50"
>
🔤 55. pattern
You can use a regular expression for browser-side validation.

Example:

<input
    type="text"
    pattern="[A-Za-z]+"
>
This accepts letters according to that pattern.

🧠 56. Complete Registration Form
<!DOCTYPE html>

<html lang="en">

<head>

    <meta charset="UTF-8">

    <meta
        name="viewport"
        content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
    >

    <title>Registration</title>

</head>

<body>

    <main>

        <h1>Create Account</h1>

        <form>

            <div>

                <label for="name">
                    Full Name
                </label>

                <input
                    type="text"
                    id="name"
                    name="name"
                    required
                    minlength="3"
                >

            </div>

            <br>

            <div>

                <label for="email">
                    Email
                </label>

                <input
                    type="email"
                    id="email"
                    name="email"
                    required
                >

            </div>

            <br>

            <div>

                <label for="password">
                    Password
                </label>

                <input
                    type="password"
                    id="password"
                    name="password"
                    required
                    minlength="8"
                >

            </div>

            <br>

            <div>

                <label for="age">
                    Age
                </label>

                <input
                    type="number"
                    id="age"
                    name="age"
                    min="18"
                    max="100"
                    required
                >

            </div>

            <br>

            <div>

                <label for="city">
                    City
                </label>

                <select
                    id="city"
                    name="city"
                    required
                >

                    <option value="">
                        Select city
                    </option>

                    <option value="delhi">
                        Delhi
                    </option>

                    <option value="jaipur">
                        Jaipur
                    </option>

                    <option value="patna">
                        Patna
                    </option>

                </select>

            </div>

            <br>

            <div>

                <p>Gender</p>

                    <input
                        type="radio"
                        name="gender"
                        value="male"
                    >
                    Male
                </label>

                <label>
                    <input
                        type="radio"
                        name="gender"
                        value="female"
                    >
                    Female
                </label>

                I agree to the terms.

            <br><br>

🧪 57. Form Dry Run
Suppose user enters:

Field	Value
Name	Aamir
Email	aamir@example.com
Password	********
Age	24
City	Jaipur
Gender	Male
Terms	Checked
Flow:

User enters data
      ↓
Browser validation
      ↓
required?
      ↓
email valid?
      ↓
age valid?
      ↓
password length?
      ↓
Submit
🌐 58. Form action and method
Important backend concept.

<form
    action="/register"
    method="POST"
>
action
Where the form data goes.

method
How data is sent.

Common:

GET
POST
🔵 GET vs POST
GET	POST
Retrieve data	Send/create data
Parameters commonly in URL	Data commonly in request body
Bookmarkable	Usually not represented by URL
Should not be used for passwords	Suitable for sensitive form submission when HTTPS is used
Example:

<form action="/search" method="GET">
versus:

<form action="/register" method="POST">
🖥️ 59. HTML + FastAPI
A form can communicate with your backend.

HTML
 │
 │ HTTP Request
 ▼
FastAPI
 │
 ├── Validate
 ├── Business logic
 ├── Database
 └── ML model
 │
 ▼
Response
 │
 ▼
HTML
Example HTML:

<form
    action="/predict"
    method="POST"
>

    <label for="age">
        Age
    </label>

    <input
        type="number"
        id="age"
        name="age"
        required
    >

    <button type="submit">
        Predict
    </button>

</form>
🎥 60. Audio
<audio controls>

    <source
        src="music.mp3"
        type="audio/mpeg"
    >

    Your browser does not support audio.

</audio>
🎬 61. Video
<video
    controls
    width="600"
>

    <source
        src="video.mp4"
        type="video/mp4"
    >

    Your browser does not support video.

</video>
Useful attributes:

<video
    controls
    autoplay
    muted
    loop
    poster="thumbnail.jpg"
>
⚠️ Browser autoplay policies can restrict unmuted autoplay.
🖼️ 62. Figure and Caption
Semantic image structure:

<figure>

    <img
        src="model.png"
        alt="Machine learning architecture"
    >

    <figcaption>
        Machine Learning Pipeline
    </figcaption>

</figure>
🌍 63. iframe
Used to embed another document/page or supported external content.

<iframe
    src="https://example.com"
    title="Example website"
>
</iframe>
For maps/videos, providers usually give embed code.

🧠 64. <details> and <summary>
Very useful for FAQs.

<details>

    <summary>
        What is HTML?
    </summary>

    <p>
        HTML structures web pages.
    </p>

</details>
Result concept:

▶ What is HTML?

click

▼ What is HTML?
  HTML structures web pages.
🪟 65. Dialog
Modern HTML has a native dialog element.

<dialog open>

    <h2>Hello</h2>

    <p>
        This is a dialog.
    </p>

</dialog>
JavaScript is generally used to open and close dialogs dynamically.

🏷️ 66. Data Attributes
Custom data can be stored using data-*.

<button
    data-product-id="101"
    data-category="laptop"
>
    Buy
</button>
JavaScript can later read:

data-product-id
data-category
Important
Use data-* for custom metadata, not arbitrary attributes.

🧠 67. Meta Tags
Meta tags provide information about the document.

Character encoding
<meta charset="UTF-8">
Responsive viewport
<meta
    name="viewport"
    content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
>
Description
<meta
    name="description"
    content="AI/ML Engineer portfolio"
>
📱 68. Responsive Viewport
This is extremely important.

<meta
    name="viewport"
    content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
>
Meaning:

Mobile screen
     ↓
Use device width
     ↓
Page scales correctly
Without a proper viewport configuration, mobile layouts can behave incorrectly.

🔍 69. SEO Basics
SEO = Search Engine Optimization.

Easy meaning:

Making your website easier for search engines and users to understand.

Important HTML elements:

<title>
<meta description>
<h1>
<h2>
semantic HTML
<img alt="">
links
Example:

<head>

    <title>
        Aamir Hussain Azad | AI/ML Engineer
    </title>

    <meta
        name="description"
        content="AI/ML Engineer portfolio featuring Python, machine learning and AI projects."
    >

</head>
♿ 70. Accessibility
Accessibility means:

Making websites usable by people with different abilities.

Important practices:

Use labels
<label for="email">
    Email
</label>

<input
    id="email"
    type="email"
>
Use alt text
<img
    src="profile.jpg"
    alt="Profile photograph"
>
Use semantic HTML
<nav>
<main>
<section>
<article>
<footer>
Use buttons for actions
Good:

<button>
    Submit
</button>
Don't use a random <div> as a button unless you have a strong reason and implement proper keyboard/accessibility behavior.

🧠 71. HTML Entities
Some characters have special meanings.

Example:

&lt;
&gt;
&amp;
&quot;
&nbsp;
Meaning:

Entity	Character
&lt;	<
&gt;	>
&amp;	&
&quot;	"
&nbsp;	non-breaking space
Example:

<p>
    5 &lt; 10
</p>
🧠 72. Character Encoding
Use:

<meta charset="UTF-8">
UTF-8 allows the document to represent a very large range of characters.

Example:

<p>Hello नमस्ते 🌐</p>
🧱 73. Block vs Inline
Block-level behavior
Examples:

div
p
h1
section
article
header
footer
Concept:

┌────────────────────────────┐
│ Block                      │
└────────────────────────────┘
┌────────────────────────────┐
│ Another block              │
└────────────────────────────┘
Inline behavior
Examples:

span
a
strong
em
Concept:

Text [link] [important] continues
⚠️ Modern CSS layout can change how elements participate in layout, so don't treat “block vs inline” as an absolute visual rule.

🧠 74. DOM — Very Important
DOM = Document Object Model

Easy meaning:

Browser converts HTML into a tree of objects that JavaScript can work with.

HTML:

<body>

    <h1>Hello</h1>

    <p>Welcome</p>

</body>
DOM:

Document
   │
   └── html
        │
        ├── head
        │
        └── body
             │
             ├── h1
             │    └── "Hello"
             │
             └── p
                  └── "Welcome"
🔥 75. DOM Parent-Child Relationship
<div>

    <p>
        Hello
    </p>

</div>
Tree:

div
 │
 └── p
      │
      └── Hello
Therefore:

div = parent
p   = child
Hello = text node
🧠 76. HTML Parsing
Browser process:

HTML file
    ↓
Parser
    ↓
DOM Tree
    ↓
CSS + layout
    ↓
Rendering
    ↓
Web page
Very simplified mental model:

HTML
 ↓
DOM

CSS
 ↓
CSSOM

DOM + CSSOM
 ↓
Render information
 ↓
Pixels on screen
🧩 77. Global Attributes
Many HTML elements can use common attributes.

Examples:

id
class
title
style
hidden
lang
dir
data-*
🆔 78. id
Unique identifier within a document.

<section id="about">
Use for:

anchors
JavaScript targeting
CSS targeting
Example:

<a href="#about">
    About
</a>

<section id="about">
    <h2>About Me</h2>
</section>
🏷️ 79. class
Used to group elements.

<p class="highlight">
    Hello
</p>

<p class="highlight">
    World
</p>
Unlike id, the same class can be used on many elements.

🧠 Remember
id = one unique identity
class = reusable group

📝 80. title
<button title="Submit your form">
    Submit
</button>
Provides advisory information, often shown as a tooltip by browsers.

Don't rely on title as the only accessibility label.

🌍 81. lang
<html lang="en">
For Hindi content:

<html lang="hi">
This helps browsers, screen readers, search engines, and other tools understand the document language.

🔐 82. HTML Security Concepts
HTML itself does not make an application secure.

Important concepts:

XSS
Cross-Site Scripting.

Easy meaning:

An attacker gets unwanted JavaScript executed in another user's browser.

Example danger:

User input
 ↓
HTML output
 ↓
Unsafe script
 ↓
Browser executes it
Prevent with proper output encoding/sanitization and secure application architecture.

🔐 83. Never Trust Client-Side Validation
Suppose:

<input
    type="number"
    min="18"
>
This is useful.

But an attacker can bypass browser validation.

Therefore:

HTML validation
       ↓
User experience

Backend validation
       ↓
Actual security
⭐ Remember
Frontend validation improves UX. Backend validation protects the application.

📁 84. File Upload
<form
    action="/upload"
    method="POST"
    enctype="multipart/form-data"
>

    <label for="file">
        Upload file
    </label>

    <input
        type="file"
        id="file"
        name="file"
    >

    <button type="submit">
        Upload
    </button>

</form>
Important:

enctype="multipart/form-data"
is required for traditional multipart file uploads.

🧠 85. HTML Comments vs Hidden Input
A comment:

<!-- secret -->
is still sent as part of the HTML source.

A hidden input:

<input
    type="hidden"
    name="user_id"
    value="123"
>
is also visible to the client.

🚨 Important
Never put secrets, passwords, private API keys, or authorization decisions in HTML.

Here's a strong HTML-only foundation for a portfolio.

🧠 88. HTML Attribute Cheat Sheet
Attribute	Easy meaning
id	Unique identity
class	Group/category
src	Resource location
href	Link destination
alt	Image alternative text
title	Advisory information
name	Form field name
value	Form value
placeholder	Example hint
required	Must provide
disabled	Cannot interact
readonly	Cannot edit
checked	Checkbox/radio selected
selected	Option selected
min	Minimum
max	Maximum
step	Increment
pattern	Validation pattern
target	Link browsing target
loading	Loading behavior
width	Width
height	Height
🧠 89. Difficult Words → Easy Meaning
Difficult	Easy
Markup	Tags describing content
Semantic	Meaningful
Attribute	Extra information
Element	Complete HTML unit
DOM	HTML represented as a tree
Accessibility	Usable by different people
Responsive	Works across screen sizes
Metadata	Information about information
Validation	Checking input
Encoding	Representing data correctly
Rendering	Turning code into visible page
Parser	Software that understands HTML
SEO	Helping search engines understand content
Endpoint	Destination of an API request
Client	User/browser/application making request
Server	Computer/application responding
Multipart	Request containing multiple data parts
Entity	A meaningful thing/content object
Semantic HTML	HTML whose tags explain meaning
🎨 90. Colorful Revision Notes
🟢 BASIC
HTML creates structure.

🔵 IMPORTANT
id should identify one unique element; class is reusable.

🟡 INTERVIEW
Semantic HTML improves structure, accessibility, and machine understanding.

🔴 SECURITY
Never trust HTML/JavaScript validation alone.

🟣 AI/ML
HTML can provide the frontend while FastAPI handles prediction/API logic.

🟠 PERFORMANCE
Use appropriate image dimensions, lazy loading where useful, and avoid unnecessary resources.

🧠 91. HTML Memory Tricks
Trick 1 — Web Stack
H-C-J

H = HTML → Structure
C = CSS → Style
J = JavaScript → Behavior
Trick 2 — Page Structure
Remember:

H-N-M-S-A-F

H → Header
N → Nav
M → Main
S → Section
A → Article/Aside
F → Footer
Trick 3 — Form
Remember:

L-I-B

L = Label
I = Input
B = Button
Trick 4 — Image
Remember:

S-A

S = src
A = alt
Trick 5 — Table
Remember:

T-R-C

T = Table
R = Row
C = Cell
More accurately:

table
 ↓
tr
 ↓
th / td
🧪 92. Common Mistakes
❌ Mistake 1
<img src="photo.jpg">
Better:

<img
    src="photo.jpg"
    alt="Profile photograph"
>
❌ Mistake 2
Using many <br> tags for layout.

<br>
<br>
<br>
<br>
Use CSS for spacing.

❌ Mistake 3
Using <div> everywhere.

❌

<div class="header"></div>
<div class="nav"></div>
<div class="footer"></div>
Prefer semantic elements where appropriate:

<header></header>
<nav></nav>
<footer></footer>
❌ Mistake 4
Duplicate IDs.

❌

<div id="card"></div>
<div id="card"></div>
Use classes for repeated elements:

<div class="card"></div>
<div class="card"></div>
❌ Mistake 5
No labels.

❌

<input type="email">
Better:

<label for="email">
    Email
</label>

<input
    id="email"
    type="email"
>
❌ Mistake 6
Putting secrets in HTML.

Never put:

API keys
Passwords
Private tokens
Database credentials
into HTML.

🎯 93. HTML Interview Questions
Q1. What is HTML?
HTML is a markup language used to structure content on web pages.

Q2. Is HTML a programming language?
No. HTML is a markup language. It describes document structure rather than implementing general-purpose programming logic.

Q3. What is an HTML element?
An HTML element is a complete unit consisting of tags and content when applicable.

Example:

<p>Hello</p>
Q4. What is an attribute?
An attribute provides additional information about an HTML element.

<a href="page.html">
href is an attribute.

Q5. Difference between id and class?
id identifies a specific element; class represents a reusable group of elements.

Q6. What is semantic HTML?
HTML elements that communicate the meaning or role of their content.

Examples:

<header>
<nav>
<main>
<section>
<article>
<footer>
Q7. What is the DOM?
DOM stands for Document Object Model. It represents an HTML document as a tree of objects that scripts can interact with.

Q8. Difference between <div> and <span>?
Traditionally, <div> is a generic block container and <span> is a generic inline container. Their actual layout can be changed with CSS.

Q9. Difference between <strong> and <b>?
<strong> communicates strong importance semantically; <b> is primarily used to draw attention without that same semantic meaning.

Q10. Difference between <em> and <i>?
<em> represents emphasis; <i> represents text set apart from normal prose for a different voice or convention.

Q11. Why is alt important?
It provides alternative text for images, helping when images cannot be displayed and improving accessibility.

Q12. What does DOCTYPE do?
It tells the browser which document mode/HTML standard expectations to use. In modern HTML:

<!DOCTYPE html>
Q13. What is responsive design?
Design that adapts appropriately to different screen sizes and devices.

Q14. What is the viewport meta tag?
<meta
    name="viewport"
    content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0"
>
It helps control the viewport used for responsive layouts on mobile devices.

Q15. GET vs POST?
GET is generally used to retrieve data and POST is generally used to submit/create/process data. Security does not come from choosing POST alone; HTTPS and server-side controls are required.

Q16. What is required?
It tells the browser that the input must have a value before the form can be submitted normally.

Q17. What is placeholder?
<input
    placeholder="Enter your name"
>
It provides a temporary hint. It should not replace a proper label.

Q18. What is readonly?
<input
    value="Aamir"
    readonly
>
The user generally cannot edit the field through normal UI, but its value can still be submitted depending on the form.

Q19. Difference between disabled and readonly?
readonly	disabled
User cannot normally edit	User cannot interact
Can generally be submitted	Disabled controls are generally not submitted
Focus behavior differs	Usually excluded from normal interaction
Q20. What is accessibility?
Making a website usable by people with different abilities and assistive technologies.

🏆 94. Most Conceptual Interview Question
Why should we use semantic HTML instead of <div> everywhere?
Answer:

Semantic HTML communicates the purpose of content to browsers, developers, search engines, and assistive technologies. It improves document structure, accessibility, maintainability, and can support SEO.

Example:

❌

<div class="header">
Better:

<header>

🎨 CSS MASTERY — COMPLETE STUDY GUIDE
Beginner → Advanced | Concepts + Code + Diagrams + Practice + Interview
CSS is the language that controls how HTML looks and how elements are laid out.

🧠 Master formula:
HTML = Structure → CSS = Appearance/Layout → JavaScript = Behavior

   mobile-first
        │
        ▼
     ADVANCED
        │
   variables
   calc()
   clamp()
   transitions
   transforms
   animations
   pseudo-elements
   functions
        │
        ▼
    REAL WORLD
        │
   Navbar
   Cards
   Forms
   Dashboard
   Portfolio
   Responsive UI
🧠 2. What is CSS?
CSS = Cascading Style Sheets

Easy meaning
CSS tells the browser how HTML elements should look and where they should appear.

Example:

HTML
<h1>Hello Aamir</h1>
CSS
h1 {
    color: blue;
    font-size: 40px;
}
Result:

       HTML
        ↓
   ┌─────────────┐
   │ Hello Aamir │
   └─────────────┘
        ↑
       CSS
   color + size
🎯 3. CSS Syntax
Basic CSS:

selector {
    property: value;
}
Example:

p {
    color: red;
    font-size: 20px;
}
Break it:

p
│
└── selector

color
│
└── property

red
│
└── value
A complete line:

color: red;
is called a declaration.

🧠 4. Important CSS Vocabulary
Difficult word	Easy meaning
Selector	Element you want to style
Property	What you want to change
Value	Setting of that property
Declaration	Property + value
Rule	Selector + declarations
Cascade	Rules used to decide final style
Specificity	How strongly a selector targets something
Inheritance	Some properties pass from parent to child
Responsive	Works on different screen sizes
Pseudo-class	Special state of an element
Pseudo-element	Virtual part of an element
Viewport	Visible browser area
Layout	Arrangement of elements
Flexbox	One-dimensional layout system
Grid	Two-dimensional layout system
🎨 5. Three Ways to Add CSS
1. Inline CSS
<p style="color: red;">
    Hello
</p>
🟡 Avoid for large applications.
2. Internal CSS
<head>

<style>

p {
    color: red;
}

</style>

</head>
3. External CSS ⭐
HTML:

<link
    rel="stylesheet"
    href="style.css"
>
CSS:

p {
    color: red;
}
🟢 Best practice
Use external CSS for maintainable projects.

🌳 6. CSS Relationship With HTML
HTML
 │
 ├── h1
 ├── p
 ├── img
 └── button
       │
       ▼
     CSS
       │
 ┌─────┼─────────┐
 ▼     ▼         ▼
Color Size      Layout
🎯 7. Selectors
Selectors are one of the most important CSS topics.

Element Selector
p {
    color: blue;
}
Selects all <p> elements.

Class Selector
HTML:

<p class="important">
    Hello
</p>
CSS:

.important {
    color: red;
}
. means class.

ID Selector
<p id="main-text">
    Hello
</p>
CSS:

#main-text {
    color: green;
}
# means ID.

🧠 8. Selector Memory Trick
Remember:

tag     → p
class   → .
id      → #
Example:

p {}
.card {}
#header {}
🔥 9. Universal Selector
* {
    margin: 0;
    padding: 0;
}
* means all elements.

🔗 10. Group Selector
h1,
h2,
p {
    color: blue;
}
Means:

h1 → blue
h2 → blue
p  → blue
🧩 11. Descendant Selector
HTML:

<div class="card">

    <p>Hello</p>

</div>
CSS:

.card p {
    color: red;
}
Meaning:

Select <p> elements inside .card.

Diagram:

.card
  │
  └── p
      ↑
    selected
🔗 12. Child Selector
.card > p {
    color: red;
}
> means direct child.

.card
  │
  ├── p       ← selected
  │
  └── div
       │
       └── p ← not selected
➡️ 13. Adjacent Sibling
h2 + p {
    color: red;
}
Means:

Select the first <p> immediately after <h2>.

➡️ 14. General Sibling
h2 ~ p {
    color: red;
}
Means:

Select later sibling <p> elements after <h2>.

🧠 15. Attribute Selectors
input[type="email"] {
    border: 2px solid blue;
}
Other examples:

input[required] {}

a[href] {}

input[type="text"] {}
🎯 16. Pseudo-Classes
Pseudo-class represents a state or condition.

Examples:

button:hover {
    background: black;
}
Common:

:hover
:focus
:active
:visited
:checked
:disabled
:first-child
:last-child
:nth-child()
:not()
🖱️ 17. :hover
button:hover {
    background-color: black;
    color: white;
}
Flow:

Normal
  ↓
Mouse enters
  ↓
:hover
  ↓
New style
⌨️ 18. :focus
input:focus {
    border-color: blue;
    outline: 2px solid lightblue;
}
Useful for keyboard and form interaction.

🧠 19. :nth-child()
li:nth-child(2) {
    color: red;
}
Selects the second child.

Example:

1 → normal
2 → 🔴 selected
3 → normal
4 → normal
🚫 20. :not()
p:not(.special) {
    color: gray;
}
Means:

Select paragraphs that do NOT have .special.

🧩 21. Pseudo-Elements
Pseudo-elements represent a specific virtual part of an element.

Common:

::before
::after
::first-letter
::first-line
::selection
Example:

h1::before {
    content: "🔥 ";
}
🧠 22. ::before and ::after
.card::before {
    content: "";
}
Important:

content is normally required for generated content with ::before and ::after.

🎨 23. Colors
CSS supports many color formats.

Named color
color: red;
HEX
color: #ff0000;
RGB
color: rgb(255, 0, 0);
RGBA
color: rgba(255, 0, 0, 0.5);
HSL
color: hsl(0, 100%, 50%);
🧠 24. HEX
Format:

#RRGGBB
Example:

#FF0000
│ │ │
│ │ └── Blue
│ └──── Green
└────── Red
📏 25. CSS Units
Important units:

px
%
em
rem
vw
vh
vmin
vmax
🔵 26. px
Fixed CSS pixel unit.

font-size: 20px;
🟢 27. %
Relative to an appropriate containing/reference size.

width: 50%;
If parent content width is 1000px:

50% = 500px
🟣 28. rem
Relative to the root element's font size.

If:

html {
    font-size: 16px;
}
Then:

2rem
means:

2 × 16px = 32px
Formula
rem value = rem number × root font size

🟠 29. em
Relative to the relevant inherited/computed font size depending on the property.

For simple font-size examples:

.parent {
    font-size: 20px;
}

.child {
    font-size: 1.5em;
}
Then:

1.5 × 20px = 30px
⚠️ Nested em calculations can compound, so rem is often easier for predictable typography.

📱 30. vw and vh
vw = viewport width.

width: 50vw;
vh = viewport height.

height: 50vh;
Formula:

1vw = 1% of viewport width

1vh = 1% of viewport height

If viewport width = 1200px:

50vw = 600px
📦 31. CSS Box Model ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
One of the most important CSS concepts.

Every normal box can be understood as:

┌───────────────────────────────┐
│            MARGIN             │
│  ┌─────────────────────────┐  │
│  │         BORDER          │  │
│  │  ┌───────────────────┐  │  │
│  │  │      PADDING      │  │  │
│  │  │  ┌─────────────┐  │  │  │
│  │  │  │   CONTENT   │  │  │  │
│  │  │  └─────────────┘  │  │  │
│  │  └───────────────────┘  │  │
│  └─────────────────────────┘  │
└───────────────────────────────┘
Remember:

Content → Padding → Border → Margin

📐 32. Box Model Formula
With default content-box:

Total width
Total Width =
Content Width
+ Left Padding
+ Right Padding
+ Left Border
+ Right Border
+ Left Margin
+ Right Margin
More precisely, the border-box width excludes margins.

If:

content = 300px
padding-left = 20px
padding-right = 20px
border-left = 2px
border-right = 2px
Then border-box width:

300 + 20 + 20 + 2 + 2
= 344px
Margin is outside that box.

⭐ 33. box-sizing
Most modern projects use:

* {
    box-sizing: border-box;
}
With:

box-sizing: border-box;
the declared width includes:

content + padding + border
Example:

.card {
    width: 300px;
    padding: 20px;
    border: 2px solid black;
    box-sizing: border-box;
}
The border-box remains 300px wide.

🧠 34. Margin vs Padding
Padding
Space inside the border.

Border
┌───────────────┐
│   PADDING     │
│   Content     │
└───────────────┘
Margin
Space outside the border.

        MARGIN
   ┌─────────────┐
   │   Border    │
   │   Content   │
   └─────────────┘
        MARGIN
Memory trick
Padding = inside
Margin = outside

📐 35. Margin Shorthand
margin: 10px;
All sides.

margin: 10px 20px;
Meaning:

top/bottom = 10px
left/right = 20px
margin: 10px 20px 30px;
Meaning:

top    = 10px
left/right = 20px
bottom = 30px
margin: 10px 20px 30px 40px;
Clockwise:

TOP
RIGHT
BOTTOM
LEFT
🧠 Trick
TRBL = Top Right Bottom Left

📦 36. Width and Height
.box {
    width: 300px;
    height: 200px;
}
Responsive:

.box {
    width: 100%;
    max-width: 600px;
}
📌 37. min-width, max-width
.container {
    width: 100%;
    max-width: 1200px;
}
This is extremely common for responsive layouts.

🎯 38. Display Property
Important values:

block
inline
inline-block
none
flex
grid
🧱 39. display: block
div {
    display: block;
}
Conceptually:

┌──────────────────┐
│ Element          │
└──────────────────┘

┌──────────────────┐
│ Next Element     │
└──────────────────┘
➖ 40. display: inline
span {
    display: inline;
}
Conceptually:

Text [element] [element] continues
📦 41. inline-block
Allows inline flow while permitting box dimensions.

button {
    display: inline-block;
    width: 150px;
    padding: 10px;
}
🚫 42. display: none
.hidden {
    display: none;
}
The element is removed from the layout.

Compare:

visibility: hidden;
This normally hides the element but preserves its layout space.

📍 43. Position
CSS position values:

static
relative
absolute
fixed
sticky
🟢 44. position: static
Default positioning.

.box {
    position: static;
}
Normal document flow.

🔵 45. position: relative
.box {
    position: relative;
    top: 20px;
}
The element is positioned relative to its normal position.

It can also establish a containing block for absolutely positioned descendants.

🔴 46. position: absolute
.child {
    position: absolute;
    top: 0;
    right: 0;
}
Usually positioned relative to the nearest positioned ancestor.

Example:

.parent {
    position: relative;
}

.child {
    position: absolute;
    top: 10px;
    right: 10px;
}
Diagram:

Parent
┌──────────────────────────┐
│                    Child │
│                       ┌──┤
│                       │  │
│                       └──┘
└──────────────────────────┘
📌 47. position: fixed
Fixed relative to the viewport.

.navbar {
    position: fixed;
    top: 0;
    width: 100%;
}
Common for:

fixed navigation
floating buttons
support widgets
📌 48. position: sticky
header {
    position: sticky;
    top: 0;
}
It behaves like normal flow until a scroll threshold is reached, then sticks within its containing scroll context.

Common for:

sticky headers
table headings
sidebars
🧠 49. Position Memory Trick
static   → normal
relative → move from itself
absolute → position within containing block
fixed    → viewport
sticky   → scroll + stick
🏆 50. Flexbox ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Flexbox is a one-dimensional layout system.

Easy meaning:

Flexbox arranges items along one main axis at a time.

.container {
    display: flex;
}
🧠 51. Flexbox Diagram
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│              CONTAINER               │
│                                      │
│   ┌─────┐   ┌─────┐   ┌─────┐      │
│   │  A  │   │  B  │   │  C  │      │
│   └─────┘   └─────┘   └─────┘      │
│                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────┘
            MAIN AXIS →
🎯 52. flex-direction
.container {
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: row;
}
Options:

row
row-reverse
column
column-reverse
↔️ 53. justify-content
Controls distribution along the main axis.

.container {
    display: flex;
    justify-content: center;
}
Values:

flex-start
center
flex-end
space-between
space-around
space-evenly
↕️ 54. align-items
Controls alignment along the cross axis.

.container {
    align-items: center;
}
Memory trick
justify = main axis
align = cross axis

📏 55. gap
.container {
    display: flex;
    gap: 20px;
}
Creates spacing between flex/grid items.

🔥 56. Perfect Centering
One of the most asked CSS problems.

.container {
    display: flex;
    justify-content: center;
    align-items: center;
}
For a fixed-height container:

.container {
    min-height: 100vh;

    display: flex;

    justify-content: center;
    align-items: center;
}
Diagram:

┌──────────────────────────────┐
│                              │
│                              │
│          ┌────────┐          │
│          │ CENTER │          │
│          └────────┘          │
│                              │
│                              │
└──────────────────────────────┘
🧩 57. Flex Item Properties
Important:

flex-grow
flex-shrink
flex-basis
flex
order
align-self
Example:

.item {
    flex: 1;
}
This commonly allows items to share available free space.

🧠 58. flex
Shorthand:

flex: grow shrink basis;
Example:

.item {
    flex: 1 1 200px;
}
Meaning:

grow   = 1
shrink = 1
basis  = 200px
🏆 59. CSS Grid ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Grid is a two-dimensional layout system.

Use it for:

rows + columns
Example:

.container {
    display: grid;
    grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr);
    gap: 20px;
}
Diagram:

┌────────┬────────┬────────┐
│   A    │   B    │   C    │
├────────┼────────┼────────┤
│   D    │   E    │   F    │
├────────┼────────┼────────┤
│   G    │   H    │   I    │
└────────┴────────┴────────┘
🧠 60. Flexbox vs Grid
Flexbox	Grid
One-dimensional	Two-dimensional
Row OR column	Rows AND columns
Components	Page/layout structure
Content-driven layouts	Structured layouts
Memory trick
Flex = line
Grid = table

📐 61. fr Unit
grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr 1fr;
Means three flexible tracks sharing available space.

Example:

Available width = 900px

1fr + 1fr + 1fr

Each ≈ 300px
🔥 62. Responsive Grid
Very useful:

.grid {
    display: grid;

    grid-template-columns:
        repeat(
            auto-fit,
            minmax(250px, 1fr)
        );

    gap: 20px;
}
Meaning:

Create as many columns as fit, while keeping each item at least about 250px wide and allowing it to grow.

This is a very powerful responsive pattern.

📱 63. Media Queries
Responsive design:

@media (max-width: 768px) {

    .container {
        flex-direction: column;
    }

}
Meaning:

Screen <= 768px
       ↓
Apply these CSS rules
📱 64. Mobile-First CSS
Recommended strategy:

Mobile
  ↓
Tablet
  ↓
Desktop
Base:

.container {
    display: block;
}
Then:

@media (min-width: 768px) {

    .container {
        display: flex;
    }

}
🧠 65. Responsive Formula
A useful mental model:

Responsive UI
=
Flexible widths
+
Relative units
+
Flexible layout
+
Media queries
+
Responsive images
🖼️ 66. Responsive Images
img {
    max-width: 100%;
    height: auto;
}
Meaning:

Image should not normally overflow its container.

✍️ 67. Fonts
body {
    font-family: Arial, sans-serif;
}
Common properties:

font-family
font-size
font-weight
font-style
line-height
letter-spacing
📏 68. font-size
h1 {
    font-size: 3rem;
}
🖋️ 69. font-weight
h1 {
    font-weight: 700;
}
Common values:

400 → normal
700 → bold
↕️ 70. line-height
p {
    line-height: 1.6;
}
Easy meaning:

Controls vertical spacing between lines of text.

↔️ 71. letter-spacing
h1 {
    letter-spacing: 2px;
}
📝 72. Text Alignment
h1 {
    text-align: center;
}
Options:

left
center
right
justify
✨ 73. Text Decoration
a {
    text-decoration: none;
}
Other values:

underline
overline
line-through
none
🌈 74. Backgrounds
.card {
    background-color: lightblue;
}
Image:

.hero {
    background-image: url("hero.jpg");
}
🎨 75. Background Properties
.hero {
    background-image: url("hero.jpg");

    background-size: cover;

    background-position: center;

    background-repeat: no-repeat;
}
cover
The image covers the area while preserving its aspect ratio; some cropping may occur.

🟣 76. Gradients
Linear gradient:

.hero {
    background:
        linear-gradient(
            90deg,
            blue,
            purple
        );
}
Radial:

.hero {
    background:
        radial-gradient(
            circle,
            white,
            blue
        );
}
🧱 77. Borders
.card {
    border: 1px solid black;
}
Shorthand:

border: width style color
Example:

border: 2px solid red;
🔵 78. Border Radius
.card {
    border-radius: 12px;
}
Circle:

.avatar {
    border-radius: 50%;
}
🌑 79. Box Shadow
.card {
    box-shadow:
        0 4px 20px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.15);
}
General conceptual format:

offset-x
offset-y
blur-radius
spread-radius
color
🧠 80. CSS Variables
Very important for professional CSS.

:root {

    --primary-color: #2563eb;

    --text-color: #111827;

    --radius: 12px;

}
Use:

button {

    background: var(--primary-color);

    color: white;

    border-radius: var(--radius);

}
🧠 81. Why CSS Variables?
Without variables:

button {
    background: blue;
}

.card {
    border-color: blue;
}

.link {
    color: blue;
}
With variables:

:root {
    --primary: blue;
}

button {
    background: var(--primary);
}

.card {
    border-color: var(--primary);
}

.link {
    color: var(--primary);
}
Change once → many places update.

🧮 82. calc()
CSS can calculate values.

width: calc(100% - 40px);
Example:

Parent width = 1000px

100% - 40px

= 960px
📏 83. min(), max(), clamp()
min()
width: min(90%, 1200px);
max()
width: max(300px, 50%);
clamp()
font-size: clamp(
    1.5rem,
    4vw,
    3rem
);
Concept:

minimum
   ↓
preferred
   ↓
maximum
🧠 84. CSS Cascade ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
"Cascading" means CSS uses rules to determine which declarations win when multiple rules apply.

Important concepts:

Origin
Importance
Specificity
Source order
Inheritance
A simplified mental model for normal author CSS:

Which rules apply?
       ↓
!important?
       ↓
Specificity
       ↓
If tied → later declaration wins
🎯 85. Specificity
A useful conceptual ranking:

Inline style
     ↓
ID
     ↓
Class / attribute / pseudo-class
     ↓
Element / pseudo-element
Example:

p {
    color: blue;
}

.text {
    color: green;
}

#main {
    color: red;
}
HTML:

<p
    id="main"
    class="text"
>
    Hello
</p>
The ID selector is more specific than the class and element selector, so red wins among these rules.

🧮 86. Specificity Score
A common teaching model is:

(a, b, c, d)
where:

a = inline styles
b = ID selectors
c = class/attribute/pseudo-class selectors
d = element/pseudo-element selectors
Example:

#box.card p
Score conceptually:

ID       = 1
class    = 1
element  = 1

(0,1,1,1)
⚠️ Don't think of specificity as simple decimal arithmetic.
Compare the categories lexicographically.

🔥 87. !important
p {
    color: red !important;
}
It changes the normal priority rules.

🔴 Avoid overusing it.
Bad:

color: red !important;
margin: 10px !important;
padding: 20px !important;
Too much !important usually means the CSS architecture needs improvement.

🧬 88. Inheritance
Some properties naturally inherit from parents.

body {
    color: #222;
    font-family: Arial;
}
Many descendant elements can inherit these properties.

Concept:

body
 │
 ├── h1 ← inherits applicable properties
 ├── p  ← inherits applicable properties
 └── a  ← inheritance depends on property/default styles
Not every property inherits.

🎬 89. CSS Transitions
Transition makes changes smoother.

button {
    background: blue;

    transition:
        background-color 0.3s ease;
}

button:hover {
    background: purple;
}
Without transition:

blue → purple
With transition:

blue → intermediate states → purple
🔄 90. Transform
.card:hover {
    transform: translateY(-5px);
}
Common transforms:

translate()
scale()
rotate()
skew()
Example:

.card:hover {
    transform: scale(1.05);
}
🎞️ 91. CSS Animation
@keyframes move {

    from {
        transform: translateX(0);
    }

    to {
        transform: translateX(200px);
    }

}

.box {
    animation:
        move 2s ease-in-out;
}
Flow:

from
 ↓
animation
 ↓
to
🔁 92. Repeating Animation
.box {
    animation:
        move 2s infinite alternate;
}
Useful properties:

animation-name
animation-duration
animation-delay
animation-iteration-count
animation-direction
animation-timing-function
animation-fill-mode
⚡ 93. CSS Functions
Important:

var()
calc()
min()
max()
clamp()
rgb()
rgba()
hsl()
url()
Example:

width: min(
    90%,
    1200px
);
🧱 94. Overflow
.box {
    overflow: hidden;
}
Other values:

visible
hidden
scroll
auto
clip
Example:

.container {
    overflow-x: auto;
}
Useful for wide tables.

👁️ 95. Opacity
.card {
    opacity: 0.5;
}
Range concept:

0 → invisible
1 → fully opaque
🧠 96. visibility vs display
display: none;
Usually removes the element from layout.

visibility: hidden;
Usually keeps its layout space while hiding it.

📌 97. z-index
Controls stacking order when elements participate in stacking contexts where z-index applies.

.modal {
    position: fixed;
    z-index: 1000;
}
Concept:

Layer 3 → Modal
Layer 2 → Navbar
Layer 1 → Page
🟡 Important
z-index is not simply "bigger number always wins"; stacking contexts matter.

🖱️ 98. Cursor
button {
    cursor: pointer;
}
Other examples:

default
pointer
not-allowed
grab
text
🧠 99. Object Fit
For images/video:

img {
    width: 100%;
    height: 250px;
    object-fit: cover;
}
Useful values:

cover
contain
fill
none
scale-down
🎯 100. Real-World Card Component
HTML:

<div class="card">

    <img
        src="product.jpg"
        alt="Laptop"
    >

    <div class="card-content">

        <h2>Laptop</h2>

        <p>
            Powerful laptop for developers.
        </p>

        <button>
            Buy Now
        </button>

    </div>

</div>
CSS:

.card {

    width: 320px;

    border: 1px solid #ddd;

    border-radius: 16px;

    overflow: hidden;

    background: white;

    box-shadow:
        0 8px 25px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.1);

    transition:
        transform 0.3s ease;

}

.card:hover {

    transform:
        translateY(-8px);

}

.card img {

    width: 100%;

    height: 200px;

    object-fit: cover;

}

.card-content {

    padding: 20px;

}

.card button {

    width: 100%;

    padding: 12px;

    border: none;

    border-radius: 8px;

    cursor: pointer;

}

When mouse enters:

.card
 ↓
:hover

translateY(-8px)

card moves upward

🧠 107. CSS Architecture
For bigger applications, avoid putting everything into one huge file.

Example:

css/
│
├── reset.css
├── variables.css
├── base.css
├── layout.css
├── components.css
├── utilities.css
└── responsive.css
Or a component-oriented structure:

styles/
├── globals.css
├── navbar.css
├── card.css
├── form.css
├── dashboard.css
└── responsive.css
🧹 108. CSS Reset
A simple reset:

*,
*::before,
*::after {
    box-sizing: border-box;
}

html {
    scroll-behavior: smooth;
}

body {
    margin: 0;
}

img,
picture,
video,
canvas,
svg {
    display: block;
    max-width: 100%;
}

button,
input,
textarea,
select {
    font: inherit;
}
This establishes more predictable defaults.

🎨 109. Design System With Variables
:root {

    --primary: #2563eb;

    --secondary: #7c3aed;

    --text: #111827;

    --muted: #6b7280;

    --background: #f9fafb;

    --surface: #ffffff;

    --border: #e5e7eb;

    --radius-sm: 6px;

    --radius-md: 12px;

    --radius-lg: 20px;

    --shadow:
        0 10px 30px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.08);

}
Then:

.card {

    background: var(--surface);

    color: var(--text);

    border:
        1px solid var(--border);

    border-radius:
        var(--radius-md);

    box-shadow:
        var(--shadow);

}
🧠 110. Advanced CSS — Container Queries
Media queries usually react to viewport size.

Container queries can react to the size of a component's containing box.

Concept:

.card-container {
    container-type: inline-size;
}

@container (min-width: 500px) {

    .card {
        display: grid;
        grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr;
    }

}
Easy meaning:

Media query asks: How wide is the screen?
Container query asks: How wide is my container?

Very useful for reusable components.

🧠 111. Advanced CSS — aspect-ratio
.video {
    aspect-ratio: 16 / 9;
}
Formula:

aspect ratio = width / height

For 16 / 9:

width : height
16    : 9
Useful for:

videos
cards
image containers
thumbnails
🧠 112. Advanced CSS — object-position
img {
    width: 100%;
    height: 300px;

    object-fit: cover;

    object-position: center;
}
You can adjust the visible focal position.

🧠 113. Advanced CSS — Logical Properties
Instead of:

margin-left: 20px;
you can use:

margin-inline-start: 20px;
Other useful properties:

margin-inline
margin-block
padding-inline
padding-block
inset-inline
inset-block
These are useful for different writing directions and internationalization.

♿ 114. Accessibility in CSS
Don't remove focus without replacement
Avoid:

button {
    outline: none;
}
unless you provide an accessible alternative.

Better:

button:focus-visible {
    outline: 3px solid blue;
    outline-offset: 3px;
}
♿ 115. Reduced Motion
Some users prefer less animation.

Use:

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {

    *,
    *::before,
    *::after {

        animation-duration: 0.01ms;
        animation-iteration-count: 1;

        transition-duration: 0.01ms;

        scroll-behavior: auto;

    }

}
🧠 116. CSS Performance
Good practices:

Use efficient selectors
↓
Avoid unnecessary animations
↓
Animate transform/opacity when appropriate
↓
Compress images
↓
Remove unused CSS
↓
Use caching
For animations, properties such as:

transform
opacity
are often preferable to repeatedly animating expensive layout properties.

🚨 117. Common CSS Mistakes
❌ Mistake 1
Using excessive absolute positioning for layouts.

left: 200px;
top: 100px;
This can break on different screen sizes.

Better
Use:

Flexbox
Grid
responsive sizing
❌ Mistake 2
Using fixed widths everywhere.

Bad:

.container {
    width: 1200px;
}
Better:

.container {
    width: 100%;
    max-width: 1200px;
    margin-inline: auto;
    padding-inline: 20px;
}
❌ Mistake 3
Using too many !important.

❌ Mistake 4
No mobile design.

❌ Mistake 5
Removing keyboard focus.

❌ Mistake 6
Using CSS as a substitute for semantic HTML.

CSS controls presentation; HTML should communicate structure and meaning.

🧪 118. CSS Dry Run — Box Model
Given:

.box {

    width: 300px;

    padding: 20px;

    border: 5px solid black;

    margin: 10px;

}
With default content-box:

Property	Value
Content width	300px
Left padding	20px
Right padding	20px
Left border	5px
Right border	5px
Border-box width	350px
Left margin	10px
Right margin	10px
Outer occupied width	370px
Calculation:

300 + 20 + 20 + 5 + 5
= 350px

350 + 10 + 10
= 370px
🧪 119. Flexbox Dry Run
.container {

    display: flex;

    justify-content: center;

    align-items: center;

}
Suppose:

Container width = 1000px
Container height = 500px

Item width = 200px
Item height = 100px
Concept:

Horizontal:

(1000 - 200) / 2
= 400px left space

Vertical:

(500 - 100) / 2
= 200px top space
Result:

┌──────────────────────────────┐
│                              │
│                              │
│          ┌────────┐          │
│          │ ITEM   │          │
│          └────────┘          │
│                              │
│                              │
└──────────────────────────────┘
🧪 120. Grid Dry Run
grid-template-columns:
    repeat(3, 1fr);
Container:

width = 900px
Ignoring gaps for simplicity:

900 / 3
= 300px
So:

Column 1 ≈ 300px
Column 2 ≈ 300px
Column 3 ≈ 300px
🧪 121. Responsive Dry Run
.card {
    width: 100%;
}

@media (min-width: 768px) {

    .card {
        width: 50%;
    }

}
Screen	Applied rule
400px	width: 100%
600px	width: 100%
768px	width: 50%
1000px	width: 50%
🎯 122. Most Asked CSS Interview Questions
Q1. What is CSS?
CSS is a stylesheet language used to control the presentation, layout, and visual appearance of HTML/XML documents.

Q2. What is the box model?
The CSS box model describes an element as:

Content
Padding
Border
Margin
Q3. Difference between margin and padding?
Margin = outside the border.
Padding = inside the border.

Q4. What is box-sizing: border-box?
It makes the declared width/height include the element's padding and border.

Q5. What is Flexbox?
Flexbox is a one-dimensional CSS layout system for arranging items along a main axis with cross-axis alignment.

Q6. What is CSS Grid?
Grid is a two-dimensional layout system for rows and columns.

Q7. Flexbox vs Grid?
Flexbox = one dimension.
Grid = two dimensions.

Q8. What is specificity?
Specificity is the selector-priority mechanism used when multiple applicable declarations compete for the same property.

Q9. Which is more specific?
p {}
.card {}
#main {}
Generally:

#main
  ↓
.card
  ↓
p
Q10. What is inheritance?
Some CSS properties can take their value from the parent element when no overriding value is specified.

Q11. What is a pseudo-class?
It represents a state or condition.

Example:

button:hover {}
Q12. What is a pseudo-element?
It represents a conceptual part of an element.

Example:

p::first-letter {}
Q13. Difference between display: none and visibility: hidden?
display: none removes the element from normal layout; visibility: hidden generally preserves its layout space.

Q14. What is position: absolute?
It removes the element from normal flow and positions it relative to its containing block.

Q15. What is position: fixed?
It positions an element relative to the viewport in the common case and keeps it fixed during normal page scrolling.

Q16. What is position: sticky?
It behaves like a relatively positioned element until a scroll threshold is reached, then sticks within its scroll container.

Q17. What is z-index?
It controls stacking order within the relevant stacking contexts.

Q18. What is responsive design?
Designing a UI so it adapts appropriately to different viewport sizes and devices.

Q19. What is a media query?
A CSS condition that applies styles when specified environmental conditions are true.

Example:

@media (max-width: 768px) {}
Q20. What are CSS variables?
Reusable custom properties:

:root {
    --primary: blue;
}
Use:

color: var(--primary);
🔥 123. Most Conceptual Interview Question
Why does this not necessarily work?
.parent {
    display: flex;
    align-items: center;
}

.child {
    height: 100%;
}
Because height: 100% needs a definite containing height in the relevant context.

Better:

.parent {
    min-height: 500px;

    display: flex;

    align-items: center;
}
Lesson
Percentage dimensions depend on the relevant containing block and whether the reference dimension is definite.

🧠 124. Another Important Question
How do you center something?
Modern answer:

.container {
    display: grid;
    place-items: center;
}
or:

.container {
    display: flex;
    justify-content: center;
    align-items: center;
}
🏆 125. CSS Problem-Solving Patterns
Problem: Center an element
display: flex;
justify-content: center;
align-items: center;
Problem: Create 3 columns
display: grid;
grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr);
Problem: Responsive cards
grid-template-columns:
    repeat(
        auto-fit,
        minmax(250px, 1fr)
    );
Problem: Image fitting
img {
    width: 100%;
    height: 100%;
    object-fit: cover;
}
Problem: Sticky header
header {
    position: sticky;
    top: 0;
}
Problem: Overlay badge
.card {
    position: relative;
}

.badge {
    position: absolute;
    top: 10px;
    right: 10px;
}
Problem: Responsive typography
h1 {
    font-size:
        clamp(
            2rem,
            5vw,
            4rem
        );
}
🧠 126. Ultimate CSS Memory System
Remember:

S-B-D-P-F-G-R-A
S → Selectors
B → Box Model
D → Display
P → Position
F → Flexbox
G → Grid
R → Responsive
A → Advanced
Advanced:

Variables
Functions
Transitions
Transforms
Animations
Container Queries
Accessibility
Performance

🧠 130. Final CSS Cheat Sheet
CSS
│
├── SELECTORS
│   ├── element
│   ├── class
│   ├── id
│   ├── attribute
│   ├── descendant
│   ├── child
│   ├── sibling
│   ├── pseudo-class
│   └── pseudo-element
│
├── BOX MODEL
│   ├── content
│   ├── padding
│   ├── border
│   ├── margin
│   └── box-sizing
│
├── LAYOUT
│   ├── display
│   ├── position
│   ├── overflow
│   └── z-index
│
├── FLEXBOX
│   ├── direction
│   ├── justify
│   ├── align
│   ├── gap
│   └── flex
│
├── GRID
│   ├── columns
│   ├── rows
│   ├── gap
│   ├── areas
│   └── fr
│
├── RESPONSIVE
│   ├── %
│   ├── rem
│   ├── vw/vh
│   ├── media queries
│   ├── clamp
│   └── container queries
│
├── VISUAL
│   ├── color
│   ├── background
│   ├── border
│   ├── shadow
│   └── gradient
│
├── TYPOGRAPHY
│   ├── font
│   ├── size
│   ├── weight
│   ├── line-height
│   └── letter-spacing
│
└── ADVANCED
    ├── variables
    ├── calc
    ├── min/max
    ├── transitions
    ├── transforms
    ├── animations
    ├── accessibility
    └── performance
🏁 CSS Master Formula
S → B → L → F → G → R → A → P
S = Selectors
B = Box Model
L = Layout
F = Flexbox
G = Grid
R = Responsive
A = Advanced CSS
P = Projects
And the most important practical rule:

🟢 Don't solve layout problems with random margin, top, left, or position: absolute. First ask: “Should this be Flexbox, Grid, normal flow, or responsive sizing?”

Once you master Box Model → Cascade/Specificity → Flexbox → Grid → Responsive Design → Variables → Animations → Accessibility, you have the core CSS foundation needed to move from HTML into JavaScript, React, FastAPI, and full-stack AI/ML applications.
